- Claim: The book was first sent only to Carmels, in place of the usual death circular. Carmels lent it and requests poured in. Pauline, not prioress, was put in charge of shipping. She had at least five orders every day.

### F-EW-1748 — Print and mail figures up to 1910
- Claim: By 1910 there were 62,815 copies of the full Life printed and 80,000 of the abridged Life; sold: 45,715 full and 56,405 abridged. From July 1909 to July 1910 the Carmel received requests for 183,348 pictures and 36,612 "souvenirs", and 9,741 letters. The daily average of letters had reached 50. Records were kept from January 1909 on the advice of Mgr de Teil.
- When / where: 1898–1910, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Mère Agnès; Mère Marie-Ange de l'Enfant-Jésus; Mgr Roger de Teil (vice-postulator)

### F-EW-1749 — Critics among Carmels: "too young to affirm her views"
- Claim: Opposition: When Histoire d'une âme came out (1898), two or three Carmels sent objections: so young a nun should not have stated her views on perfection so absolutely; age would have changed them; the prioress should not have let her write so, and still less published it. Another prioress (dead by 1910) said that in speaking of her graces she might be simple, but one could also see pride. Pauline says the critics later changed their minds.
- When / where: 1898–1899
- People: two or three unnamed Carmels; an unnamed prioress
- Witness: Mère Agnès; from letters received
- Source: PO 1, f. 295v–296r, Session 19, 2 Sept 1910

- Translation: "one could also see pride in it."

- Notes: The critic Carmels are not named.

- Claim: After Story of a Soul appeared in October 1898, letters began to arrive. Around 1911 they averaged 50 a day, then 200, 300 and 400 a day; since August 1914, some days more than 500.

### F-EW-1751 — Print runs 1898–1915 and the relic sachets
- Claim: From 1898 to 1915: 211,515 full Story of a Soul; 710,000 abridged Lives; 111,000 "Pluie de roses"; 8,046,000 image-portraits; 1,124,200 souvenir sachets. Translations into 35 languages or dialects. In four years (1911–1915) the Carmel bought 146,724 metres of ribbon for 1,760,000 sachets (containing bits of cloth that had touched her), costing 88,000 francs. In one year (July 1914–July 1915) 472,000 sachets; in under a year, 2,291,000 portraits. More than 100 portable altars for soldier-priests were given in her name through La Croix.
- When / where: 1898–1915
- People: Lisieux Carmel
- Witness: Mère Agnès
- Source: PA 6, [526]–[527], S21 19 Jul 1915

- Confidence: high

### F-EW-1752 — Three prioresses objected to Story of a Soul
- Claim: When Story of a Soul appeared (1898), three Carmelite prioresses made remarks. The prioress of the rue d'Enfer Carmel in Paris said some of Thérèse's statements would have been changed by age and experience; soon after she completely changed her mind. The prioress of the avenue de Messine (Paris) thought the Life childish and in contrast with Carmel's austerity. Pauline believes the prioress of the avenue de Saxe thought the same. All three are now dead, and their communities admire Thérèse.

### F-EW-1842 — Scale of the cult 1909–1916
- Claim: MT, in charge of letters and of orders for books and pictures, gives figures: 1,830 letters of thanks for graces counted in 1909; about 100 letters a day in 1910; 350–400 a day in July 1914. The four volumes of "Pluie de roses" (to 1913) have 1,365 pages with 1,488 graces. In 13 months 1,002,300 medals went out; from July 1915 to July 1916, 4,118,500 pictures were printed. The Carmel held 460 marble ex-voto plaques, 14 Legion of Honour crosses and 33 war crosses from soldiers; 240 portable altars for army priests were given in her name; 87,500 Masses were requested Jan–Sept 1916; 780 francs came in one month for candles before the Virgin of the Smile; 600 press cuttings came via the "Argus de la Presse".

- Witness: MT, her own work

### F-EW-1888 — Céline versus the Promoter of the Faith
- Claim: At the Process, the Promoter of the Faith asked Céline why she wanted the beatification. She said: only to make the Little Way known. She says she felt more joy at Benedict XV's discourse on spiritual childhood (14 Aug 1921) than at the beatification and canonisation.
- When / where: Process (1910–1917); 14 Aug 1921
- People: Céline; the Promoter of the Faith (not named)
- Witness: Céline — her own account
- Source: CS §II "Esprit d'enfance"

- Translation: "If you speak of a 'way', the Cause will certainly fall."

- Confidence: medium — Céline's later memory of her own exchange
- Notes: Process fact. Shows pressure on witnesses and Céline's resistance.

### F-EW-2448 — Bishop Giannattasio: who he was and how he testified
- Claim: Nicola Giannattasio was born at Bisceglie on 17 Jan 1871 and ordained priest on 21 Dec 1893. He became Bishop of Nardò on 30 Nov 1908 (consecrated 31 Dec 1908). Nardò borders the diocese of Gallipoli. He testified in Italian on 21–22 Aug 1911 (sessions 86–87, f. 1309v–1329v). The promoter of the faith, M. Dubosq, translated his words into French at once. Later he refused the see of Otranto, became titular archbishop of Pessinus, lived in Rome, and died at Frascati on 24 Aug 1959.
- When / where: 21–22 Aug 1911, Bayeux–Lisieux tribunal
- People: Nicola Giannattasio; Dubosq (promoter of the faith)
- Witness: editors' introduction (Carmel site), not the witness himself
- Source: PO 33, introduction and headings [1310v]–[1311r], W1; rights: facts-only
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The editors also note that he knew Dante, was a friend of the composer Perosi, and published Saggi di apologia cattolica (1916). The court used a French translation, so the French text is not the witness's own wording.
- Extraction ID: N-F01
### F-EW-2449 — He testifies only on Gallipoli, and says his desire is for God's glory
- Claim: The bishop said he came mainly to speak of the "prodigious facts" at the Carmel of Gallipoli (Articles 145, 145a, 145b). He said he knew them by personal knowledge and would name reliable witnesses where he used them. He said he had strong devotion to the Servant of God and wished her cause to succeed "for no human feeling", only for God's glory and the edification of souls in the present time. For questions 10–26 (her life) he said he knew nothing in particular.
- When / where: 21 Aug 1911, session 86
- People: Giannattasio
- Witness: Giannattasio
- Source: PO 33, [1311r]–[1311v], W1
- Original words: « Je sais de science personnelle ce que je rapporterai sur ces faits. »
- Translation: "What I will report on these facts I know by personal knowledge."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: He never met Thérèse. His evidence covers the cult and the Gallipoli events, and his view of her teaching.
- Extraction ID: N-F02
### F-EW-2450 — "A new way" of holiness: Francis of Assisi, Francis de Sales, Teresa
- Claim: The bishop judged her holiness "among the most eminent in the Church" and "characteristic": in Providence's plan it traces a new way to perfection. Asked what "new" means, he said it is like a man of genius who gives a new way to grasp an unchanging truth. He named St Francis of Assisi, St Francis de Sales and St Teresa as "typical" saints who lit up one virtue of the Gospel by their example, not only their teaching. In his view Thérèse became a "new exemplary cause" of holiness. The one authoritative cause of all holiness stays the same.
- When / where: 21 Aug 1911, session 86
- People: Giannattasio; Francis of Assisi; Francis de Sales; Teresa of Avila
- Witness: Giannattasio (opinion, not eyewitness)
- Source: PO 33, [1311v]–[1312r], W1

- Claim: Asked what her special way is, the bishop said: love of God seen as love of trust and abandonment, and the practice of "little acts" of the virtues. ### F-EW-2454 — How the bishop heard the Gallipoli story: 6–7 Feb 1910, in the parlour
- Claim: Bishop Gaetano Müller of Gallipoli invited Giannattasio on 6 Feb 1910 to pontificate for St Agatha, patron of Gallipoli. Müller told him of the 16 Jan event a few days after it happened. On 7 Feb 1910 Müller led him to the Carmel parlour and ordered the prioress, Mother Carmela of the Sacred Heart of Jesus, to tell it. She was very reluctant. ### F-EW-2455 — The prioress hesitates to say the word "bilocation"
- Claim: Giannattasio noted that the prioress hesitated much before repeating the word "bilocation". She said it only after Bishop Müller pressed her, ordering her to tell the whole truth. ### F-EW-2457 — The apparition's caress and the first guess that it was St Teresa
- Claim: In the account the prioress gave, the visitor put her hand on the prioress's head and set the folds of her veil straight "as a caress". The prioress first thought she saw St Teresa, the foundress, and cried "O Santa Madre". The visitor answered: "I am not the Santa Madre; I am the Servant of God Sister Thérèse of Lisieux. Today is a feast in heaven and on earth." The witness adds that it was the feast of the Holy Name of Jesus (16 Jan 1910).
- When / where: 16 Jan 1910, Carmel of Gallipoli
- People: Mother Carmela; Thérèse (as reported); Teresa of Avila
- Witness: Giannattasio, repeating the prioress
- Source: PO 33, [1315v]–[1316r], W1
- Original words: « Je ne suis pas la "Santa Madre", je suis la Servante de Dieu sœur Thérèse de Lisieux »
- Translation: "I am not the 'Santa Madre'; I am the Servant of God Sister Thérèse of Lisieux."
- New to English readers: partial — the story is known; the veil caress and the feast day detail come from this deposition
- Confidence: medium — second-hand
- Notes: Liturgy and connection-to-saints item.
- Extraction ID: N-F10
### F-EW-2458 — The sacristans, the drawer, and "one must not believe in dreams"
- Claim: At Mass that morning the two sacristans saw the prioress was pale and wished to put her to bed and call the doctor. She told them she was still under the effect of something felt in the night that she thought was a dream, and briefly told them about the box in the drawer. She said, as if mocking them, that one must not believe in dreams. The sisters, moved by a kind of religious fear, dared not open the drawer. The prioress had to open it herself and found the 500 livres.
- When / where: 16 Jan 1910, morning, Gallipoli
- People: Mother Carmela; two sacristans (unnamed)
- Witness: Giannattasio, repeating the prioress
- Source: PO 33, [1316v]–[1317r], W1
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium — second-hand
- Notes: The prioress got up "by a great effort" after a night of pleurisy suffering to take communion.
- Extraction ID: N-F11
### F-EW-2459 — Testing the natural explanations (sleepwalking, hidden money, fraud)
- Claim: Asked whether the prioress might have been a sleepwalker or nervous, so that she put money already in the house into the box, the bishop called this "absolutely inadmissible". His reasons: she was of very sound mind, then and later; the monastery was in extreme poverty and the country itself poor, "little religious and little generous", with no benefactor for such sums; the creditor had pressed for 16 days; and Bishop Müller's very severe inquiry found no natural explanation. Giannattasio said Müller would have seized any pretext to end the affair.
- When / where: Aug 1911 (testimony on events of Jan 1910)
- People: Giannattasio; Müller; Mother Carmela
- Witness: Giannattasio
- Source: PO 33, [1317r]–[1317v], W1
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high that he said it; the conclusion is his own opinion
- Notes: Interesting for the "opponents" scope: the local bishop began as a sceptic.
- Extraction ID: N-F12
### F-EW-2460 — Later sums: the bishop did not verify them and gives them less weight
- Claim: The bishop said that for the events from 16 Jan 1910 to 16 Jan 1911 (surplus in the accounts) he did not give them the same weight, because he had not checked them himself. The prioress said she found surpluses that had no natural cause. Müller checked her accounts often and carefully, and told Giannattasio the source of the sums was inexplicable to him. Müller had put her through "so many trials, examinations and vexations" that she gained nothing by lying. Müller also said the whole community's spiritual progress was extraordinary and coincided with the events; Giannattasio confirmed this from directing their retreats.
- When / where: 1910–1911, Gallipoli
- People: Giannattasio; Müller; Mother Carmela
- Witness: Giannattasio
- Source: PO 33, [1318r]–[1318v], W1
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: A candid limit on his own evidence.
- Extraction ID: N-F13
### F-EW-2461 — Why he sent 500 livres, and the timing of the Christmas gift
- Claim: The bishop had a special devotion to the Servant of God: he asked her, and had the Carmelites ask her, to help with hard problems in running his diocese. To please her, he thought of giving the Gallipoli Carmel 500 livres, the same sum as in the apparition, on the anniversary date. He hesitated because Gallipoli was not his diocese. On Christmas day 1910 a benefactor gave him 1,000 livres to use as he wished. He said at once: "Sister Thérèse comes to my aid", and hesitated no more.
- When / where: Christmas 1910
- People: Giannattasio; unnamed benefactor
- Witness: Giannattasio
- Source: PO 33, [1318v]–[1319r], W1
- Original words: « Sœur Thérèse vient à mon aide »
- Translation: "Sister Thérèse is coming to my help."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Extraction ID: N-F14
### F-EW-2462 — The sealed envelope: how he prepared it (28 Dec 1910)
- Claim: On 28 Dec 1910 a trusted person from Gallipoli visited him. Alone and unseen, he put a 500-livre note into a small open envelope with his visiting card. On the card he wrote "In memoriam... My way is sure, and I was not mistaken in following it" (Sister Thérèse of Lisieux to Sister Maria Carmela, 16 Jan 1910) and "Pray for me every day". He wrote "In memoriam" on the small envelope. He put it in a double "security" envelope (inner blue thin paper, outer stronger and lighter), sealed with wax and his coat of arms. Outside he wrote: to be put in the usual small box and opened by the prioress on 16 Jan 1911. He told the messenger only to take care of it.
- When / where: 28 Dec 1910, Nardò
- People: Giannattasio; an unnamed messenger from Gallipoli
- Witness: Giannattasio
- Source: PO 33, [1319r]–[1319v], W1
- Original words: « Ma voie est sûre, et je ne me suis pas trompée en la suivant »
- Translation: "My way is sure, and I was not mistaken in following it."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: He stresses that the words "In memoriam" could not be read through the double envelope, and that the messenger did not know the contents.
- Extraction ID: N-F15
### F-EW-2463 — 16 Jan 1911: the prioress will not open the envelope
- Claim: On 16 Jan 1911, at about 4 p.m., the bishop went to the Carmel parlour by Müller's order to set the timetable of the eight-day retreat he was to preach. The prioress said the envelope was still unopened in the box and asked him to open it. She had written him earlier: "Do not fail to come yourself on the 16th; it seems to me the Servant of God wants to give you a sensible, though indirect, sign of her supernatural action." He thought she wished to avoid new trouble and public attention. A few days before, she had seen that the envelope had grown thicker.
- When / where: 16 Jan 1911, Carmel parlour, Gallipoli
- People: Giannattasio; Mother Carmela; Müller
- Witness: Giannattasio
- Source: PO 33, [1323v]–[1324v], W1; session 87, 22 Aug 1911
- Original words: « il me semble que la Servante de Dieu veut vous donner un signe sensible »
- Translation: "It seems to me the Servant of God wants to give you a sensible sign."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high that he said it; her motives are his reading
- Notes: The prioress's own reserve is again clear: she kept away from the miracle.
- Extraction ID: N-F16
### F-EW-2464 — The nuns' prayer for altar flowers: 300 livres
- Claim: For a year the nuns had planned to ask Sister Thérèse for a set of flowers for the altar of their poor chapel, a more "spiritual" good than the debt. They estimated the cost at 300 livres. The prioress first refused to join the request, then agreed and prayed for it with the others. Giannattasio linked this prayer to what he found in the envelope.
- When / where: 1910–Jan 1911, Gallipoli
- People: Gallipoli Carmelites; Mother Carmela
- Witness: Giannattasio (from the prioress)
- Source: PO 33, [1324r]–[1324v], W1
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium — hearsay
- Extraction ID: N-F17
### F-EW-2465 — Opening the envelope: 500 livres in, 300 livres extra found
- Claim: The prioress tore one corner of the strong, well-gummed envelope and opened it with her finger. Then, without touching the contents, she handed it through the grille: "Take it, since it is yours." The bishop saw more notes than the 500 he had put in: two of 100 and two of 50 livres in the second, thin envelope. His small envelope lay in the middle. His first thought was that his 500 note had been changed for smaller notes. The prioress said: count, perhaps this is the 300 the sisters asked for. She offered to call the sisters and have him give the sum "as from himself". One 50-livre note gave off a light smell of roses. He took it for himself and replaced it with another note of the same value.
- When / where: 16 Jan 1911, Gallipoli
- People: Giannattasio; Mother Carmela; the community
- Witness: Giannattasio
- Source: PO 33, [1325r]–[1325v], W1
- Original words: « un des billets de cinquante livres répandait une légère odeur de rose »
- Translation: "one of the fifty-livre notes gave off a light smell of roses."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high that he testified it; single eyewitness for the scent
- Notes: Existing cards give the total (300 in four notes, one scented). This card adds the bishop's own words on how the notes lay and what he did with the scented note. He gave the community the sum without speaking of a miracle.
- Extraction ID: N-F18
### F-EW-2466 — He checks his seal and the gum folds
- Claim: The prioress said quickly: check if it is really your seal. He examined the returned envelope closely. The seal was intact. He also knew, he said, particular marks of how he had gummed the flaps himself with a brush and liquid gum, which left small folds; he found them identical. His own 500 note and card were intact inside among the other notes.
- When / where: 16 Jan 1911, Gallipoli
- People: Giannattasio; Mother Carmela
- Witness: Giannattasio
- Source: PO 33, [1325v]–[1326r], W1
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The witness's own tamper test. No one else checked.
- Extraction ID: N-F19
### F-EW-2467 — His reaction: tears in his room that evening
- Claim: He gave the retreat talk at once and put aside his impressions. That evening, alone in his room at the bishop's house in Gallipoli, he reflected freely and was "convinced to the point of evidence" that the event was supernatural. He was "moved to tears". He rejected the idea that the prioress put the notes in: she could not have such a sum, the envelopes were intact, and she was incapable of fraud. He said all her nuns vouched for her, and Müller (a priori against the supernatural in these events) also affirmed her moral character.
- When / where: 16 Jan 1911, evening, Gallipoli
- People: Giannattasio; Müller
- Witness: Giannattasio
- Source: PO 33, [1326r]–[1326v], W1
- Original words: « j'en fus ému jusqu'aux larmes »
- Translation: "I was moved to tears."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high that he said it
- Extraction ID: N-F20

### F-EW-2469 — What "My way is sure" means: the bishop's argument
- Claim: The bishop said he chose the card's words on purpose. From the first he thought the phrase "very important", and regretted that the church inquiries had not drawn out its meaning. In his view it means: the spiritual way to holiness that she followed and taught is sure. He rejected a material reading ("the way through the corridors is known to me"). His arguments: "mia via" would not be understood in the physical sense, especially in Italian, and "seguire la via" is used mainly in a figurative sense.
- When / where: 22 Aug 1911, session 87
- People: Giannattasio
- Witness: Giannattasio
- Source: PO 33, [1327r]–[1327v], W1
- Original words: « La mia via è sicura, e non mi sono sbagliata seguendola »
- Translation: "My way is sure, and I was not wrong in following it."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Other witnesses (see M-EW-080) may leave the physical sense open, since she offered to guide the prioress out. Card links to the corridor detail in F-EW-2457/2456.

- Claim: At the end, he corrected a small ambiguity in Article 145b (p. 162), which came from a poor translation of his Italian report to the vice-postulator. The sentence that Giannattasio "then did not know the words" is true only for the time when he formed the plan to send the alms. It is not true of the time when he actually sent it, because he had learned that detail shortly before.
- When / where: 22 Aug 1911, session 87
- People: Giannattasio; vice-postulator (Mgr de Teil)

- Source: PO 33, [1329r]–[1329v], W1

- Claim: Giannattasio said he owed to her protection extraordinary spiritual graces in the difficult government of his diocese. ### F-EW-2473 — Mère Agnès hands the court an analysis of 54 miracle dossiers
- Claim: Mère Agnès said she had not personally seen any miraculous cure. She said the letters that reached the Carmel were full of "more or less miraculous" favours, and some were printed in the "Pluies de roses". She had prepared a short analysis of 54 of these reports and gave it to the tribunal, with the original files. The court checked the text and put it in the Acts. She read it aloud.

### F-EW-2474 — Where the 54 cases come from
- Claim: The 54 cases in Mère Agnès's summary come from France (many departments), Italy (Florence, Palermo, Ceprano), Spain (Tàrrega, Zaragoza), Ireland, Scotland, Canada (Rogersville, Montréal), Cuba, Mexico, Bohemia, Serbia, Syria, Austria, India (Mangalore) and Madagascar. - Claim: Dr Hérébel of Lambezellec certified on 28 May 1914 the cure of Philomène Le Gouez's tuberculous thigh ulcer within one month. ### F-EW-2485 — The voice of June 1908: "One gives oneself to Love only as far as to suffering"
- Claim: Céline said that in June 1908, on her great retreat for the anniversary of her profession, she had again given herself to Love. She asked how she would know she was fully given. About 4 a.m. she woke to a sound of words and someone said in her ear: "One gives oneself to Love only so far as one gives oneself to suffering." She did not doubt it was the Servant of God. She understood that the gift is not one general act but a work of each day and each moment.

- People: Céline; Thérèse
- Witness: Céline
- Source: PO 4, f. 410r–v, S34, W1

### F-EW-2486 — Céline did not believe in the perfumes, then smelled roses in Thérèse's own cell
- Claim: Céline said that at the end of 1908 people began to speak of perfumes by which Thérèse made her presence known. She rebelled against this: she called it "coarse" because it pleased the senses, and concluded she did not believe in it. One evening near the end of Nov 1908, entering "our cell, which is the very one Sister Thérèse occupied", she found the oratory before the cell filled with a scent of roses. She stayed unbelieving ("now they perfume everything; it may be a letter soaked in scent"), though she knew there was none there, and left without checking. On the turn of the stairs, far from the cell, she felt "a breath" of roses. Then she believed and wanted to thank Thérèse, but it vanished at once.
- When / where: late Nov 1908, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Céline; Thérèse
- Witness: Céline
- Source: PO 4, f. 410v, S34, W1
- Original words: « je le disais grossier, parce qu'il frappait agréablement les sens »
- Translation: "I called it coarse, because it pleased the senses."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high that she testified it; single witness
- Notes: A surprise: a sister of Thérèse who was a sceptic about the perfumes. She shared Thérèse's cell in 1910.
- Extraction ID: N-F39
### F-EW-2487 — Rules Céline saw in the perfumes: since 1908 only, and never when wanted
- Claim: Céline said similar events happened to her about fifteen times in the two years after Nov 1908. She noted that the perfumes did not occur in the eleven years after Thérèse's death, only from 1908. Apart from two or three times when the scent lasted a long time, it vanished as soon as she understood it was Sister Thérèse. They always underlined a particular fact or were sent to console her in pain. She was never given them when she wished, but always when she was thinking least about it. Only after some moments did it occur to her it was Thérèse.
- When / where: Nov 1908 – Sep 1910, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Céline
- Witness: Céline
- Source: PO 4, f. 410v–411r, S34, W1
- Original words: « je n'en ai jamais été favorisée quand je le désirais »
- Translation: "I was never favoured with them when I wished for them."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high that she testified it
- Notes: An early observer's list of conditions. Compare M-EW-023/024 for other witnesses on perfumes in the house.
- Extraction ID: N-F40
### F-EW-2488 — The coffin plank that smelled of incense (15 Sep 1910)
- Claim: After the exhumation of 6 Sep 1910 a board from the head of the coffin was said to have come loose but was not brought back. On Thursday 15 Sep 1910 the prioress sent a portress to gather wood fragments and look for that board. She took a board from a hedge that looked like it, unsure of its identity. The porter-sister put it on a table. Sr Marie de la Trinité came in for a parcel, knew nothing of the board, and was struck by a strong smell of incense. She said to herself "surely it is from the coffin". Two other nuns (Sr Marie de l'Enfant-Jésus and Sr Thérèse de l'Eucharistie) smelled it too. The prioress and Céline, though warned, smelled nothing "near or far".
- When / where: 15 Sep 1910, Lisieux Carmel

- Claim: The Florence Carmel of Santa Maria Maddalena de' Pazzi made one of two Italian translations of Histoire d'une âme. Its prioress sent copies to many Italian bishops and had warm letters back. ### F-EW-2503 — A country-by-country survey of the letters (Belgium to Oceania)
- Claim: Isabelle listed the letters by country. Belgium: "almost as well known as in France"; a report by Fr Robert, Oblate of Mary Immaculate (letter 184). Spain: Carmels honour her, notably St Joseph of Avila; many ask for a Spanish translation. Portugal: fewer letters; the edition by the Jesuit Fr Santanna was granted indulgences by 13 bishops (letters 208–216). Germany: from 1899–1900 Princess Arnulf of Bavaria begged to translate the Life. Poland: the Polish translation was reprinted several times. Holland: the Engelen community of Our Lady of Lourdes spread images. Constantinople, Africa, India, China, Japan, Canada, the USA, South America, Oceania: letters in ranges. England: the most ardent. Africa: the White Fathers honour her; Fr Bellière, whom she called her spiritual brother, wrote (no. 356); Fr Hugueny OP of the Biblical School in Jerusalem also wrote (no. 302).

- Source: PO 16, [639v]–[642r], W1

### F-EW-2505 — Isabelle's own words on reading the Life in autumn 1901
- Claim: Isabelle read the book in early autumn 1901. She said she was "conquered from the first pages by the purity and simplicity of this soul". She kept the memory of "heavenly hours, full of light, refreshment and peace". She had dreamed of Spain's Carmels; this gave way to a new dream, to be a Carmelite at Lisieux. She thought that after such a saint's passage, Lisieux must be the most fervent convent in the world. She noted every word "as if the Holy Spirit himself had spoken", and felt led to make the Offering to Merciful Love. She entered "despite many obstacles". At Lisieux the "time of spiritual sweetness" had passed, and Thérèse trained her by the way of self-denial.
- When / where: autumn 1901 – 1911
- People: Isabelle; Thérèse
- Witness: Isabelle
- Source: PO 16, [649r]–[649v], W1

### F-EW-2507 — Conflict: how the Gallipoli-like "reservoir" was told (Isabelle vs Marie-Madeleine)
- Claim: Isabelle tells the kitchen reservoir story as follows. The stove tank holds four and a half jugs. Marie-Madeleine had emptied it. As she began to pour, the tank was full, with no one having entered. Isabelle puts it "in 1910, about Lent, I think". Marie-Madeleine's own deposition (PO 18) puts it in the summer of 1910 and says four jugs. In both, only about one jug had been poured.

### F-EW-2528 — Saints that Marie des Anges compared her with: Mechtild, Gertrude, Margaret-Mary
- Claim: MdA said that in Thérèse's heart divine love was a fire hidden under the ashes of the Carmel's hidden life. She wondered whether Our Lord, who had already chosen St Mechtild, St Gertrude and Bl. Margaret-Mary to show the world his love, would use the Servant of God to set the earth on fire. She quoted words of Jesus to St Mechtild (that it pleases him when men expect great things of him) as an explanation of the wonders Thérèse works. She also quoted a priest of Argentina (Barréro, 1914) who called the posthumous apostolate a renewed Pentecost.

### F-EW-2531 — Marie des Anges's own perfumes and the veil with thirty boils
- Claim: MdA said she smelled perfumes several times: once while preparing small papers for relics; her sister Jeanne-Marie smelled the same when she opened the box of papers. More than two years before 1915, at the moment of Communion, an exquisite perfume enveloped her; for some days she smelled violets on leaving the altar rail. Later events of trial or consolation for her family explained them. She also told that Céline, unable to tell which was Thérèse's great veil, asked Thérèse for a sign: the veil she laid on the diseased leg of a white-veil sister covered with about thirty boils would heal her. The sister was cured, and from that day (7 years before, on 1 June) she cooked alone, a task usually shared out by turns among the white-veil sisters.

- People: Marie des Anges; Jeanne-Marie; Céline; Sr Marie-Madeleine (the white-veil sister, unnamed here)

### F-EW-2540 — Pichon: three features of her "way"
- Claim: Pichon named three things that struck him in her spiritual way: (1) her spirit of faith, from childhood used to see God in everything, even the smallest things; (2) her spirit of trust and love: in the most painful trials she saw only pledges of God's love, and her heart was always ready to bless God, and she did not deny God's gifts out of "sulking humility" but admitted them to thank him; (3) a "cult" of suffering and the cross, made of esteem and love, far above her age.

### F-EW-2555 — Perfumes and a thorn: TSA's signs from Thérèse (violets, narcissi, the thorn of 2 Jan 1911)

### F-EW-2557 — TSA's last days: the prayer at Thérèse's infirmary, and her death at 11:30 p.m.

### F-EW-2563 — An extern sister on portraits: 'she is too humbled lying there; she is so great now'
- Claim: After the Beatification (1923), Sr Marie-Élisabeth pictured Thérèse in such glory that she said: 'One can never make enough beautiful portraits of our holy little Thérèse; but lying down in the cloister near the infirmary, a month before her death, she is too humbled there. I do not like people of the world to see her so; she is so great and so glorious now.' The prioress (Agnès) answered that this intimate photograph might encourage some sufferers more than an apotheosis, and that Our Lord let himself be shown on the Cross. Marie-Élisabeth did not seem convinced. She was too frail to go to Rome for the Beatification and wept when others went.

- Claim: From about 1920 the extern sister suffered inner torment and scruples for about fifteen years. ### F-EW-2575 — 'The Virtue Coach': Marie's game-piece, led by 'an angel: Thérèse of the Child Jesus'
- Claim: The circular prints one of Marie's last recreation compositions, 'La Vertu', a royal coach of a Bride of Christ driving up to perfection by the road of renunciation: two horses (Joy and Generosity), shoes for the road (love of suffering, humiliations, contempt, oblivion), coachman Love, whip Penance, brake Fear, lackey Charity, two axles (Regularity, Resignation), wheels (Fidelity, Fervour; Abandonment, Confidence), springs (Gentleness, Condescension, good character), lanterns (spiritual direction), a cushion (Peace), a carpet (Humility), the roof 'Desire of Heaven'. It ends: the coach is preceded by an angel, 'Thérèse of the Child Jesus', and is caught up like Elijah's chariot 'in a whirlwind of fire: merciful Love'. The rules include: a ticket is 'good will'; do not tighten the brake too much.

### F-EW-2576 — Marie's objection to being called 'benefactress': she would rather be humbled than break the Rule
- Claim: Two years after profession Marie fell ill and could not follow the Rule fully. A sister tried to console her by saying the community regarded her as a benefactress. This troubled her, and she told Agnès only after Jesus had consoled her: 'I am willing to be the community's little benefactress by drawing down Heaven's blessings by my virtues, but not to have a right to fail in obedience, poverty and mortification. If I do not follow the Rule, I prefer the humiliation of it, as if I had brought nothing to the monastery.' The circular adds that she said, a few days before dying, that she had never once failed to keep the least recommendation of her prioresses.

### F-EW-2579 — Marie's last words and the Thérèse statue: 14 April 1905
- Claim: On 14 Apr 1905, Marie entered her agony in the morning. The chaplain visited at 7 a.m. and said he would offer her at the consecration of Mass at 9, as a little host; she said quickly 'And after?' — 'After, that is God's secret.' Her brother-in-law, the doctor (Dr La Néele), knelt at her bed and could speak only through tears; she asked 'Am I going to die soon?... in how long?' A sister brought 'the Virgin of Thérèse' (the statue that smiled): 'How I love her! How beautiful!' She kissed the Child Jesus statue and tried to remove a little flower from her crucifix: 'No withered flowers for Jesus.' Her last words: 'I am not afraid to die! oh what peace!... One must not fear suffering... He always gives strength... Oh how I would like to die of love!... My Jesus, I love you!' At that moment the chaplain consecrated the host at Mass. More than twenty priests and a large crowd attended her burial.
- When / where: 14 Apr 1905, Lisieux Carmel infirmary

### F-EW-2580 — Marie on Thérèse: 'I have her same abandonment; I entered the legion of little victims'
- Claim: The circular quotes Marie's words in her last illness: 'It seems to me that Thérèse communicates her sentiments to me, and that I have her same abandonment. She asked the prioress to remind her of her wish to die saying to Jesus that she loved him, if pain prevented her acts of love. ### F-EW-2642 — Marie de l'Eucharistie's souvenirs of Thérèse: a linen with her tears; the 'Ultima verba'
- Claim: Piat says that on 15 Aug 1898 Marie received as a feast gift one of the linens on which Mère Agnès had gathered the tears of the dying Thérèse. She also kept as 'Ultima verba' the talk by which Thérèse prepared her for her veiling, which she regarded as her Mistress's spiritual testament. Sr Marie des Anges took over the novices the day after Thérèse's death (30 Sept 1897) and kept them until April 1900, though Thérèse continued to be her chief influence. The 30th of the month was celebrated as a small feast: a recreation for the novices, a special meal, and at seven a little hymn composed by Mère Agnès sung to the Infant Jesus that Thérèse used to decorate with flowers.

### F-EW-2643 — Isidore Guérin publishes Story of a Soul: permission of 7 March 1898, printer, and the print run
- Claim: Piat says that once the bishop, Mgr Hugonin, gave authorisation on 7 Mar 1898, Isidore Guérin himself paid the costs, and it was on his advice and as the crowning of his efforts that the Carmel entrusted to the Imprimerie Saint-Paul at Bar-le-Duc the 'modest pages' that would become one of the great bookshop successes of modern times. He supervised the edition and read proofs with his usual probity and attention to detail. The work was printed in 2,000 copies and came off the press on 30 Sep 1898 (the official date; in reality 10 Oct), the first anniversary of her death. It was sent, in place of the usual obituary circular, to all the Carmels of France and abroad.
- When / where: 7 Mar – 10 Oct 1898, Lisieux / Bar-le-Duc

### F-EW-2644 — Marie de l'Eucharistie's letter of 21 Nov 1898: 'a spark of love' and 'the fire keeps under the ashes'
- Claim: Marie wrote to her mother on 21 Nov 1898 that she had complained to 'our Angel' that she did not feel her protection and asked Thérèse to take her by the hand again on the way of pure love, adding that her love could never be as strong as Thérèse's. Next day at prayer she thought that every soul has a spark of that love; Thérèse had started with only a spark but fed it until it became a great fire. She listed her tools: embers (small sacrifices, even good will alone), small wood, larger wood, and for bellows 'acts and sighs of love'. Next day she asked why the heart is a furnace at Communion and then grows cold. God answered: 'The fire keeps under the ashes'; her ashes would be her small daily immolations 'consumed by Love and in Love'.
- When / where: 21 Nov 1898, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Marie Guérin (Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie); Mme Guérin; Thérèse

### F-EW-2647 — Mme Guérin's death, 13 Feb 1900, and what she said about Thérèse
- Claim: Piat says Céline Guérin's health failed in 1899; a cure at Royat in July brought only superficial improvement. On 29 Jan 1900 influenza brought her to the gates of death. Two days earlier her son-in-law, Dr La Néele, in a desperate effort, put mustard poultices on her legs; the reaction was atrocious. After a long sleepless night she said: 'I suffered much but my little Thérèse watched over me tenderly. All night I felt her near my bed. Several times she caressed me, which gave me extraordinary courage.' Abbé Ducellier, curé of Saint-Pierre, who gave her the last sacraments, said: 'In twenty-five years as a priest I never saw the last aids of religion received with such angelic piety.' She held her crucifix for hours saying 'Come, my Jesus, come... how happy I am to die!' To the extern sister sent by Gonzague she said: 'I seem to see in you my little Marie and the whole Community helping me.' On 10 Feb she received Viaticum. She asked pardon of all, especially her servants; had brought her dearest relics, 'a cross Thérèse had received for her First Communion, and the profession candle of Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie'; and said 'Lord, I renew my sacrifice, I give you a second time my little Marie... I offer my life for priests, like my little Thérèse.' She died on Tuesday 13 Feb 1900, feast of the Agony of Our Lord, aged nearly 53.
- When / where: Jul 1899 – 13 Feb 1900, Lisieux
- People: Céline Guérin; Dr La Néele; Abbé Ducellier; Sr Marie-Élisabeth; Thérèse
- Witness: Fr Piat (1967), quoting Marie's and her family's letters; modern author
- Source: Extract from Fr Stéphane-Joseph Piat OFM, biography of Marie Guérin (Office Central de Lisieux, 1967), on the Carmel site, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/au-carmel-du-temps-de-therese/la-communaute/soeur-marie-de-leucharistie/biographie-de-soeur-marie-de-leucharistie/ — section 'La mort des justes'; rights: facts-only

### F-EW-2648 — After his wife's death: Isidore's 'bottomless abyss', and Jeanne's household moves in (1900)
- Claim: Isidore Guérin wrote: 'God may try us, press us, we will love him all the same.' 'At the very hour of separation I said: My God, it is a bottomless abyss that you open in my heart. Your love alone can fill it... I taste a peace, a consolation in myself that makes me almost happy... I bless suffering and I almost ask for it, because at certain moments it lets me see God.' Marie (Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie) told Marcelline Husé that her father was in constant pain but resigned; at his last parlour he said for fifteen minutes: 'O my children, ask for love... pursue it until you obtain it... and finally die of love', with sobs. On the urging of family and Marie herself, Isidore agreed to take in the household of his daughter Jeanne: Dr La Néele left his Caen practice and settled with his wife at rue Paul Banaston in 1900. Piat says this later added a worldly note to the old house.

### F-EW-2650 — Marie's tuberculosis, 1903–1905: horse-serum injections, abscesses, and a request for a phonograph disc of her father
- Claim: Piat says that at the end of July 1903 a dry cough revealed the return of the bacillus that had slept since an infectious flu ten years earlier. The tuberculosis lasted twenty-one months with alternating improvement and crisis. Marie trusted the doctors 'and they made her pay dearly': her father brought a Paris University eminence who tried a new remedy, repeated injections of horse serum, and the result was an eruption of enormous abscesses that tortured her and nearly killed her twice. She bore it with exemplary endurance. She wrote in pencil: 'I cannot tell you these six months of illness; it would be too long. I suffered much. If only I had suffered well for God!' She asked for a New Year's gift: her father's voice recorded on a phonograph disc, singing the Bluebeard air that used to frighten little Thérèse, which 'with the Story of a Soul is making its tour of the world'.
- When / where: Jul 1903 – Apr 1905, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Marie Guérin; Isidore Guérin; Dr La Néele

- Source: Extract from Fr Stéphane-Joseph Piat OFM, biography of Marie Guérin (Office Central de Lisieux, 1967), on the Carmel site, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/au-carmel-du-temps-de-therese/la-communaute/soeur-marie-de-leucharistie/biographie-de-soeur-marie-de-leucharistie/ — section 'La mort des justes'; rights: facts-only

### F-EW-2651 — Marie de l'Eucharistie on suffering: 'I am not one who desires suffering'; 'letting God act'
- Claim: Piat notes that Marie did not run to meet trial: 'I am not a soul to desire suffering, no, but to accept it when it comes and rejoice in it for God's glory.' Isidore had misread the mystical words of a poem Mère Agnès wrote for her; Marie hastened to reassure him she had not sought the cross nor called for Heaven: 'The rule of my life has always been to let the good God act... to be carefree like a small child in its mother's arms.' She guessed that her mother had arranged for her this discipline: 'Because she was humility itself and feared only one thing for her children: pride, domination over others. It was one of the last recommendations she made to our Mother (Pauline) about me: Above all, my Pauline, watch that Marie does not become proud!'

### F-EW-2653 — Marie's death, 14 April 1905: time, the Virgin of the Smile, and the impact on the community
- Claim: Piat gives Marie's death as 14 Apr 1905 'at ten fifteen in the morning', aged 34 years 7 months; her father had sent a farewell note that morning, quoted in her circular. Marie had always felt an instinctive horror of death and had begged Thérèse to help her through 'the great passage'; she was heard 'beyond all her hopes'. Mère Isabelle of the Sacred Heart, present, still spoke of it nine years later: 'When one has seen Sister Marie de l'Eucharistie die, one can no longer fear death.' The statue of the Virgin of the Smile was brought to the infirmary and Marie's face lit up: 'Oh, how beautiful she is!' She expired 'like Thérèse, in a last prayer: My Jesus, I love you!' She was buried near Thérèse in the Lisieux cemetery.

### F-EW-2654 — Agnès to Léonie, 24 April 1905: 'the chapel was too small; never seen for any ceremony'
- Claim: On 24 Apr 1905 the prioress, Agnès, wrote to Léonie (Visitation of Caen): 'Ah, dear sister, how I suffered! ... I am rather relieved now that I know my little Marie happy and delivered. ### F-EW-2658 — Isidore Guérin and the Cause: he did not encourage it, and wanted to leave town
- Claim: Piat says that Thérèse's uncle did not support the effort to introduce the Cause. He kept in his soul a cult of the ideal child whose virtues he had admired, but pictured a canonised saint with extraordinary charisms, sensational mortifications and dazzling exploits, and was too prudent to risk a trial in the Roman Court and a failure that Norman clubs and salons might mock. When the publication of the Story of a Soul and the echo of the first miracles filled Lisieux gossip, he felt irritation, and for a time thought of leaving the town. Under the influence of Jeanne and Francis La Néele he doubted the enterprise, which the worldly called 'an affair of nuns'. In 1907 a chapter of the Rain of Roses was first added to the autobiography; Piat says Isidore lived long enough to sense her rising glory.
- When / where: 1898–1909, Lisieux
- People: Isidore Guérin; Jeanne and Francis La Néele

### F-EW-2659 — 8 May 1908: Marie-Ange asks the bishop to open the Cause; 'We expected this request'

Piat says he did not know until Heaven that he had been right to call his niece, on the day he authorised her entry at fifteen, 'a privileged little flower', and that she would soon become, in the judgment of a Pope, 'the greatest saint of modern times'.

### F-EW-2660 — The Guérin family and the Holy Face: cathedral lamp, family prayer, and the Shroud of Turin
- Claim: Piat says Isidore Guérin took part in spreading the devotion to the Holy Face that the Lisieux Carmel later promoted. It arose in him from reading the life of 'the Holy Man of Tours', M. Dupont. He placed the image in the cathedral of Saint-Pierre with a lamp always kept by him, had a moving copy at home, and gathered his family for prayer before the bleeding features of Christ. When the first studies of the Turin Shroud appeared, he took a passion for them, and told his daughter at the parlour of the stir made among scientists by the photographic discoveries of the 1898 exhibition. In 1902 he bought Paul Vignon's book 'Le Linceul du Christ' and lent it to Sr Geneviève (Céline), who then put all her art into painting the Saviour's face. Three years later he had a full-size reproduction of the Shroud sent for his niece, bearing the seal of the Archbishop of Turin and Baron Antonio Mansio, president of the Sacred Art Exhibition. Piat says this was the origin of Céline's pathetic painting of the Holy Face, which drew a cry of admiration from Pius X and spread in millions of copies.
- When / where: 1898–1905, Lisieux
- People: Isidore Guérin; Céline (Sr Geneviève); Paul Vignon; Pius X; Baron Antonio Mansio
- Witness: Fr Piat (1967); modern author who interviewed Thérèse's sisters
- Source: Extract from Fr Stéphane-Joseph Piat OFM, biography of Marie Guérin (Office Central de Lisieux, 1967), on the Carmel site, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/au-carmel-du-temps-de-therese/la-communaute/soeur-marie-de-leucharistie/biographie-de-soeur-marie-de-leucharistie/ — section 'La mort des justes'; rights: facts-only
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium-high
- Notes: The photographic Shroud (Secondo Pia, 1898) leads to Céline's painting (1904–05). See M-EW-041 for Céline's Holy Face copy. Connection: Pius X's reaction.
- Extraction ID: N-F211
### F-EW-2661 — Isidore Guérin's last years and death: 'Call me, Jesus!' (28 Sept 1909)
- Claim: Piat says Isidore grew gentler in devotion: 'Oh my poor children, it is finished! I can no longer make my Way of the Cross: I weep too much, it makes me ill.' Prematurely old, he could only regret that he could no longer serve the cause by word or pen: 'Jesus knows he is my life and my all, but I weep to be so indifferent to him!... and yet I would like to die for him!' For several years he had a grave liver ailment with acute rheumatism; a violent crisis struck him down. He asked for the sacraments and the Third Order indulgence and received them fully conscious. In his torment he was heard to say: 'Call me, Jesus!', his last words. He died at home, rue Paul Banaston, on Tuesday 28 Sep 1909 at 10.45 a.m., aged 68½. The funeral on Friday 1 Oct filled the great Saint-Pierre cathedral; by his express will he went to the cemetery in the poor man's hearse, and was buried without speeches. La Dépêche de Lisieux (successor of Le Normand) published a long obituary the next day calling him 'the surest of friends, the most discreet of confidants'.

- People: Isidore Guérin

- Claim: At the Apostolic Process Céline said the years 1877–1886 were "a period of darkness… a veil thrown over those qualities". In the world Thérèse "passed unnoticed". Céline never heard a sharp word from her.

### F-LF-193 — What Marie de Gonzague told Fr Madelaine
- Claim: Fr Godefroy Madelaine (Premonstratensian of Mondaye; later abbot of Saint-Michel de Frigolet) testified that Marie de Gonzague confided that, to exercise Thérèse's virtue, she "had studiously sought to try her by affecting towards her a certain indifference and severity", knew it was painful and saw no deviation from perfect obedience (Apostolic Process p.739). ### F-LF-242 — Printing and spread of the book, 1898–1955
- Claim: Carmel figures (Claude Langlois): 1898 two runs of 1,000; 1899 4,000; 1900 2,000; 1901 2,000; 1902 3,000; 1903 4,000 (cumulative 20,000); 1906 (29,000); 1909 (40,000); 1910 5,275 (60,000); Feb 1911 30,000 (90,000); Aug 1911 (100,000); 1912 (125,000; 675 pp. with appendices); 46 editions to 1955, the last printing 10,000 in 1955. ### F-LF-246 — The "rain of roses": how devotion began
- Claim: Laveille says letters of favours arrived by hundreds and the Carmel printed them in volumes, *Pluie de roses* ("Shower of Roses"): in fifteen years seven volumes, no less than 3,000 pages; the first recorded facts go back to 1902; vol. vii covers the years between beatification and canonization. From 1898 the *Annals of the Propagation of the Faith* recorded unhoped-for mission results. A White Father in Africa wrote (Dec 1910) that he put her picture in Christian homes and catechism schools, that natives asked who this little "bikira" (virgin) was, and pagans came "in compact crowds". Père Roulland testified that she had "very remarkable influence in converting souls" in Japan, China and the Indies, and that many Trappistine nuns in Japan attributed their vocation to her. Postulants came to Lisieux from all over France, Ireland, Portugal, Italy, Constantinople and Argentina. Pius X and later popes praised the book. Laveille also quotes Maurice de Waleffe, a sceptic, in *Le Journal*: "Her short and uneventful life was that of a lily which opens at morn and silently fades at eve… She has written a book… Here we have the real miracle."
- When / where: 1898–1925; worldwide
- People: the Carmel; Fr Roulland; a White Father; Maurice de Waleffe
- Source: LAV p.402–415, p.419–420
- Source type: early-life / popular
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial (baseline: "shower of roses" title)
- Confidence: medium-high
- Notes: Laveille adds that Thérèse was named the "Strewer of Roses" and "little Queen of Alençon". He also says Canadian dioceses loved her, US cities, and in Protestant hospitals nurses passed her pictures bed to bed; also a Liverpool factory-owner gave the book to each of his hundred workers and an annual paid eight-day holiday (Shower of Roses, IV, Preface, p.8).
### F-LF-247 — Three nuns Thérèse "conquered": Marie-Ange, Isabelle, Thérèse de l'Eucharistie
- Claim: Laveille names three Lisieux nuns who entered because of the book: Mère Marie-Ange de l'Enfant Jésus, of Breton origin, "rather egoistic character" with "genuine greatness of soul", prioress at a very young age (eighteen months), who in 1908 persuaded the newly elected Bishop Lemonnier to support the Cause; she died of consumption on 11 Nov 1909. Mère Isabelle du Sacré-Cœur, poet, mistress of novices, died 31 Jul 1914 aged 33. Mère Thérèse de l'Eucharistie, born Countess de la Tour-d'Auvergne, who asked "How would our little Thérèse have acted?" A retreat priest told the prioress, "it is easy to see that a saint has lived in your Carmel."

### F-LF-248 — The Cause: dates
- Claim: Laveille gives: Mgr de Teil appointed vice-postulator (early 1909; died 20 May 1922 holding her picture). 10 Feb 1910: Sacred Congregation of Rites authorised the Process of Beatification, starting with her writings. First session of the informative process at Bayeux in Aug 1910, ended Dec 1911. 10 Dec 1912: decree approving her writings. 10 Jun 1914: Pius X signed the decree introducing the Cause. 14 Aug 1921: decree of heroic virtue (Benedict XV's discourse on "the secret of holiness"). 11 Feb 1923: decree approving her miracles (Pius XI called her "a miracle of virtues and a prodigy of miracles"). 19 Mar 1923: decree "de tuto". 29 Apr 1923: beatification. Pius XI then waived the delay. 17 May 1925: canonization; feast on 30 Sep. Laveille says he was canonized "twenty-eight years after death, two years after beatification".
- When / where: 1909–1925; Bayeux; Rome
- People: Mgr de Teil; Mgr Lemonnier; Pius X; Benedict XV; Pius XI
- Source: LAV p.413, p.420, p.426–427, p.430–440
- Source type: official (as printed by Laveille)
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial (baseline gives 1923/1925)

- Notes: Mgr de Teil once asked Thérèse in the parlour whether miracles were known for the Compiègne Carmelites; she said, "How glad one would be to have miracles to make known to him!" (LAV p.413 fn.). Laveille remembers hearing de Teil speak of her at Issy in early 1910 and had "not heard her name" since 1887.
### F-LF-249 — The exhumations (1910, 1917) and the translation of 26 March 1923
- Claim: First exhumation: 6 Sep 1910 in the presence of Mgr Lemonnier; the remains were put in another coffin in a cemented vault near the first grave. After thirteen years "nothing was found of her but her bones" (as she had foretold); the habit was thin; the bones "exhaled a sweet perfume". The palm was intact. Second exhumation: 9 and 10 Aug 1917, the first official identification. Translation: 26 Mar 1923, about fifty thousand pilgrims. When the slabs were loosened "a distinct perfume of roses" reached workmen and bystanders. The coffin went on a carriage with white drapery embroidered in silver, with an escort of over two hundred priests, twenty Catholic delegations and an American Legion guard of honour, cortège over two kilometres; no hymns, bells or trumpets (Church law forbade public cult before Rome's decision), only rosary decades and psalms. The relics were divided: Postulator Fr Rodrigo of St Francis of Paula took a portion to Rome; the major relics were put in the base of the shrine; the rest in the marble recumbent statue.
- When / where: 1910–1923; Lisieux
- People: Mgr Lemonnier; Fr Rodrigo; pilgrims
- Source: LAV p.399 fn., p.420–421, p.428–430
- Source type: official / early-life
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high for dates as printed
- Notes: Laveille cites the Album of the Fêtes (Carmel, 1923). Medical/physical: bones and clothes only; EN Wikipedia (baseline) notes 1910 exhumation.
### F-LF-250 — The war of 1914–1918: soldiers and their "little Sister"
- Claim: Laveille says soldiers (poilus) invoked her along with St Michael and Joan of Arc: "True, we have Joan of Arc… but the little Sister is nearer to us." An airman wrote that he had lost her relic and was in despair. A line soldier wrote to his mother that once he received her relic he lost his fear. An artilleryman asked her to take the place of his dead mother. Artillery batteries and aircraft were named for her, regiments consecrated to her; General Duplessis vowed a pilgrimage. The air-pilot Père Jean Bourjade (Missionary of the Sacred Heart, later missionary in New Guinea) wrote that his plane carried her picture and "on more than ten occasions" he had narrow escapes. A dying Bavarian prisoner who had lost both legs said she came down to him after a night of prayer and his wounds healed. The Carmel chapel walls were covered with crosses of the Legion of Honour, epaulettes and swords sent as ex-votos.
- When / where: 1914–1918; the Western Front; Lisieux
- People: soldiers; Père Jean Bourjade; General Duplessis
- Source: LAV p.421–425 (Interventions de Sœur Thérèse pendant la guerre, Preface; *La Croix*, 19 and 21 Feb 1925)
- Source type: popular / old-collection
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes (baseline: "soldiers of 1914–1918" named as a gap)
- Confidence: medium — collected testimonies printed by the Carmel.
- Notes: Some entries are miracle-type (see M-LF-028, M-LF-029).
### F-LF-251 — Beatification, 29 April 1923
- Claim: In St Peter's, forty-five archbishops and bishops and all ambassadors to the Holy See (led by a French delegate) were present. The Brief was read; Mgr Lemonnier, Bishop of Bayeux, intoned the Te Deum and the chandeliers were lit at once. Bernini's "Glory" showed her picture: Thérèse with arms wide in ecstasy on clouds, two angels holding scrolls "Way of Childhood" and "I will spend my heaven in doing good upon earth". In the evening Pius XI came to St Peter's, the first time a pope did so for a beatification, and he made her his "patron and counsellor" and the "Star" of his pontificate. He said, "She has in truth thrown light on questions that I was unable to solve." In the Carmel a triduum of 28–30 May 1923 was led by Cardinal Vico, papal legate; a town triduum in Aug 1923 included cardinals Bourne, Dougherty and Touchet and fifteen bishops; on 8 Aug Touchet preached to six thousand; about a hundred thousand joined the procession. At the abbey the relics halted before the statue where she consecrated herself to Mary in 1884; her old teachers sang the Magnificat. Cardinal Touchet said she had won him gratitude in the Joan of Arc cause: when it was about to be interrupted, at his prayer she caused Pius X to revoke his decision.
- When / where: 29 Apr – Aug 1923; Rome; Lisieux
- People: Pius XI; Mgr Lemonnier; Cardinal Vico; Cardinals Bourne, Dougherty, Touchet
- Source: LAV p.430–437
- Source type: official / early-life
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high for dates as printed
- Notes: Laveille says "on the very day of beatification thirty new miracles recorded". Connection to other saints: Joan of Arc (Touchet's story).
### F-LF-252 — Canonization, 17 May 1925
- Claim: In St Peter's the ambassadors of the main Catholic nations, thirty-four cardinals, over two hundred archbishops and bishops, and fifty thousand faithful were present; hundreds of thousands were in Rome. The Secretary of Briefs said, "Arise; Peter is about to speak by the mouth of Pius." The formula named 30 September as the day of her memory in the universal Church. Silver trumpets sounded and the bells of Rome rang. Pius XI called her "Star of his pontificate, Advocate of his dearest causes, second Angel guardian of his life". Laveille says the cupola, façade and colonnade were lit with thousands of torches, "a spectacle not seen since 1870". At Lisieux a High Mass began at the moment the Pope reached the altar in Rome. From 4 to 12 Jul 1925 the Bishop consecrated the enlarged chapel. On 30 Sep 1925 Cardinal Vico laid the golden rose, sent by the Pope, in the hand of the recumbent marble figure; more than thirty bishops, three cardinals and an Oriental Patriarch walked in the procession, with forty national flags; that night the town was lit around the chapel and a shining star topped the cross of the dome.
- When / where: 17 May – 30 Sep 1925; Rome; Lisieux
- People: Pius XI; Cardinal Vico; Mgr Lemonnier
- Source: LAV p.438–446
- Source type: official / early-life
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high for dates as printed
- Notes: Later titles (patron of missions 1927; secondary patron of France 1944; Doctor of the Church 1997) are in the plan baseline, not in my sources.
### F-LF-253 — People beyond the Catholic Church: Grant, Guest and others
- Claim: Laveille prints letters and reports: a former United Free Church minister of Lochranza in Arran, Rev. Alexander Grant, wrote on 23 Apr 1911 that he had read the English autobiography a year earlier, opened it at random, and said "Thérèse is here in this room"; she told him to choose her little way and to invoke Our Lady. He was baptised on 20 Apr 1911 at Edinburgh by a Jesuit, "the first member of the Free Church of Scotland" to enter the Catholic Church; he lived later at Alençon in her birth-house welcoming pilgrims, and died 19 Jul 1917. Rev. Newmann Guest, an Anglican minister, wrote to *The Universe* asking for a joint novena and a reunion of England. A Liverpool factory-owner read the book and gave it to all his workers.

### F-LF-254 — Louis and Zélie Martin: process, beatification and canonization
- Claim: Louis's diocesan process opened at Lisieux 22 Mar 1957 and closed 12 Feb 1960; Zélie's opened at Sées 10 Oct 1957 and closed 7 Jan 1959; writings approved 9 Jun 1964; the causes were joined in 1971; declared Venerable 26 Mar 1994 (John Paul II). Beatification miracle: Pietro Schilirò, born 25 May 2002 in Monza with a severe lung malformation; parents Valter and Adele; a Carmelite suggested a novena to Louis and Zélie; his condition turned on 29 Jun 2002 and he left the clinic well by 27 Jul 2002 (medium confidence). Diocesan inquiry closed at Milan 10 Jun 2003 (Card. Tettamanzi). Benedict XVI approved the miracle 3 Jul 2008 and the couple was beatified at Lisieux on 19 Oct 2008. Canonization miracle: Carmen Pérez Pons, born 15 Oct 2008 in Valencia at about six months' gestation with a grade 4 intraventricular brain haemorrhage plus infection and lung and heart problems; prayers began about 19 Oct 2008; she left intensive care on the third day and went home 2 Jan 2009. Diocesan inquiry in Valencia 7 Jan 2013 – 21 May 2013 (27 May per another page); miracle approved 18 Mar 2015; canonization by Pope Francis on 18 Oct 2015, the first married couple canonized together.
- When / where: 1957–2015; Lisieux; Sées; Monza; Valencia; Rome
- People: Louis; Zélie; Pietro Schilirò; Carmen Pérez Pons
- Source: https://louisandzeliemartin.org/history-of-the-cause ; https://www.louisandzeliemartin.org/canonization-miracle ; https://www.louisandzeliemartin.org/the-beatification-miracle ; https://therecord.com.au/news/international/extraordinary-story-of-carmen-leads-to-canonisation-for-parents-of-st-therese/ ; https://www.aciprensa.com/noticias/54764/... ; Il Giornale 19 Jul 2009 (all via helper, web-context [T10])

## Part 11 — Historical context: church and state in France (facts only)
### F-LF-258 — The laws of 1901, 1904 and 1905
- Claim: The associations law of 1 Jul 1901 came from Prime Minister Waldeck-Rousseau. FR Wikipedia says the Republic then expelled the Jesuits and, in 1903, all other teaching congregations, and in 1904 forbade religious to teach (the ban lifted only in 1940 by Vichy). The law of 9 Dec 1905, moved by Aristide Briand, ended the 1802 Concordat regime (still in force in Alsace-Moselle) and separated Churches and State; it ended state funding of any cult.
- When / where: 1901–1905; France
- People: Pierre Waldeck-Rousseau; Aristide Briand
- Source: https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loi_du_9_d%C3%A9cembre_1905_concernant_la_s%C3%A9paration_des_%C3%89glises_et_de_l%27%C3%89tat ; https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loi_du_1er_juillet_1901_relative_au_contrat_d%27association ; https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Histoire_de_la_la%C3%AFcit%C3%A9_en_France (raw page text read)
- Source type: scholarship
- Rights: facts-only
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium-high
- Notes: All these fall after Thérèse's death. They matter for the "cult" story (the causes for beatification ran in the 1910s–1920s during the aftermath). Laveille does not discuss them.
### F-OM-005 — The heroic-virtues decree answers an objection based on her childhood illness
- Claim: The 1921 decree says that of all the objections raised against the cause, one deserved special notice because, if proved, it would touch "the whole substance of the cause": the illness she suffered at ten was said to have had so harmful an effect on her and her actions that it made her holiness suspect. The decree answers: the same sworn eyewitnesses who describe the illness also describe the sudden, complete cure by the Virgin, and the law requires that both statements be believed. If traces of the illness remained, then her gentle, humble, patient, cheerful and even temper in Carmel shows even greater virtue, because she had to fight harder.
- When / where: decree of 14 Aug 1921, Rome
- People: the Devil's advocate side ("quae obiecta ex adverso fuerant"); the Patron (lawyer) of the cause, not named
- Source: AAS 13 pp. 450–451
- Source type: official | Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « De illa enim sermo est aegritudine … quaeque in eam eiusque actiones adeo maleficam suam exercuisse vim, ut suspectam ipsius redderet sanctitatem »
- Translation: "The objection concerns that illness … which is said to have exercised so harmful a power over her and her actions that it made her holiness suspect."
- Other sources: none in this family. The Promoter of the Faith was Angelo Mariani (AAS 13 p. 452).
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Reactions and opposition. Type of conflict: canonical objection by the Promoter of the Faith's side. Reason: a nervous or mental illness could undermine proof of virtue. The decree does not name the illness.
### F-OM-011 — Pius X praised the autobiography in a letter (two versions of the words)
- Claim: (a) Pius XI's brief says that "Pius X of recent memory did not hesitate to assert" that in her autobiography "the virtues shine as an example and it almost breathes the soul of the virgin of Lisieux". (b) Taylor prints Pius X's letter to the prioress of the Carmel of Florence, who had sent him her Italian translation: "This book, redolent of the virtues of Sœur Thérèse, into which indeed it may be said that her whole soul has passed, has been to us a source of most sweet joy. She has blossomed as a lily …"
- When / where: before Aug 1914 (Pius X died 20 Aug 1914); letter undated in Taylor
- People: Pius X; prioress of the Carmel of Florence
- Source: (a) AAS 15 p. 205; (b) Taylor 1924 PDF 23; Taylor 1926/30 PDF 41–42
- Source type: official (a); old-collection (b) | Rights: public-domain
- Original words (a): « nitere in exemplum virtutes et fere spirare animam Lexoviensis virginis »
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high for (a); medium for (b) (date and original not given)
- Notes: Neither text contains "the greatest saint of modern times". See F-OM-095.
### F-OM-018 — *Histoire d'une âme* went out as her death notice, October 1898
- Claim: Laveille says Mère Marie de Gonzague accepted the idea of printing the *Histoire d'une âme* and sending it to all the Carmels as the usual biographical notice; it appeared in October 1898. ## Part C. War, apostolic process, venerable (1914–1921)
### F-OM-036 — The Apostolic Process, 1915–1917
- Claim: The Carmel says the apostolic process opened on 17 Mar 1915 in the sacristy of Bayeux cathedral; an "inchoative" phase heard witnesses over 50 years old; the "continuative" phase opened on 1 Apr 1916; the tribunal was dispensed from the inquiry into her reputation for holiness because of the many soldiers' petitions; the process closed on 30 Oct 1917. Taylor says the 91st and last session was held on 30 Oct 1917 in the cathedral of Bayeux; the records filled 2,500 pages, 2,000 of them on heroic virtue; de Teil took them to Rome; on 10 Dec 1918 their validity was recognized.
- Source: Carmel "Historique"; Taylor 1926/30 PDF 274–275
- Source type: official; old-collection | Rights: unknown; public-domain
- Other sources: Plan §9 said the apostolic process "closed 25 Aug 1916" with 58 sessions. This conflicts; see gaps file.
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high for 17 Mar 1915 and 30 Oct 1917
### F-OM-037 — Soldiers' petitions to the pope, 1916–1918
- Claim: The Carmel says officer Pierre Mestre, who found *Histoire d'une âme* while stationed at Lisieux, proposed in 1916 (to P. Emmanuel Bailly, superior general of the Assumptionists) that soldiers send petitions to Benedict XV. The archive keeps 26 petitions from officers and soldiers dated July 1916 – February 1918; more than twenty were calligraphed at Lisieux. Named petitioners include Commandant Pierre Mestre (92nd Infantry), Alexandre Ginetti (Belgian soldier), Colonel de Gastines, Captain Victor Bastien and Sergeant Tabut. They asked the pope to « hâter la Béatification ». The Carmel says these few dozen petitions were decisive for the dispensation from the reputation inquiry.
- Source: Carmel, "Supplique des soldats envoyés au pape pour la béatification de Thérèse"
- Source type: official (Carmel) | Rights: unknown
- Other sources: Taylor 1926/30 PDF 273: "petitions reached the Holy Father from the battle front on behalf of the Cause of the 'Little Sister of the trenches'". Laveille PDF 424: many soldiers "pleaded the cause of their 'little Sister' with the Holy See".
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
### F-OM-039 — Second exhumation and first official identification, 9–10 August 1917
- Claim: Laveille and the Carmel say the remains were exhumed a second time on 9–10 Aug 1917 and, "for the first time officially identified". Taylor says it was meant to be private but thousands came; two medical experts identified the bones, which were placed in a carved oak chest inside a rosewood coffin lined with lead.
- When / where: 9–10 Aug 1917, Lisieux cemetery
- Source: Laveille PDF 428; Carmel "Historique"; Taylor 1926/30 PDF 274
- Source type: early-life; official | Rights: public-domain; unknown
- Other sources — minor conflict: Taylor's narrative says "August 10, 1917"; his table (PDF 318) says "August 9, 1917". Two days of work explain both.
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
### F-OM-040 — The Congregation's steps on the virtues, 1920–1921
- Claim: Antepreparatory congregation 1 Jun 1920 (Taylor); preparatory congregation 25 Jan 1921 in the Vatican (AAS 13 p. 115 Diarium; Taylor); general congregation before Benedict XV on 2 Aug 1921 (AAS 13 pp. 451, 453; bull AAS 17 p. 340).
- Source: AAS 13 pp. 115, 451, 453; AAS 17 p. 340; Taylor 1926/30 PDF 275
- Source type: official | Rights: public-domain

### F-OM-041 — Three apostolic processes on miracles (Arras, Paris, Bayonne) were validated, 28 June 1921
- Claim: At a particular congregation on Tuesday 28 Jun 1921 the Congregation examined "the validity of the three Apostolic Processes built, one in the Curia of Arras, another in that of Paris and the third in that of Bayonne, on miracles asserted to have been worked by God through the intercession of the Servant of God Teresa of the Child Jesus".
- Source: AAS 13 p. 365 (Diarium, Italian)
- Source type: official | Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes

### F-OM-042 — Decree on heroic virtues, 14 August 1921
- Claim: Benedict XV chose Sunday 14 Aug 1921 (13th Sunday after Pentecost, eve of the Assumption); after Mass he summoned Cardinal Antonio Vico (Bishop of Porto and Santa Rufina, Prefect of the Congregation and relator of the cause), Fr Angelo Mariani (Promoter General of the Faith) and the secretary Alessandro Verde, and declared that her theological and cardinal virtues were proved "in heroic degree". The decree opens by saying that among recent causes it is not easy to find another that "has stirred so much favour and grace throughout almost the whole Christian world".
- Source: AAS 13 pp. 449–452
- Source type: official | Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « aliam facile reperire non est, quae tantum favoris atque gratiae per universum paene … christianum orbem, excitare valuerit »
- New to English readers: partial (baseline has the date)
- Confidence: high
### F-OM-043 — The 1921 decree says her fame "far exceeds" her humble person
- Claim: The decree says the popularity of the cause far exceeds "the humble and modest nature" of the cause and of her person: "it concerns a girl who lived a short, hidden life in the cloister, known to few while she lived"; its fruits cannot be explained by human effort but only by divine grace; God gave this example "to this proud age, which proclaims the rule of human reason above Faith", confirming "Unless you become like little children…". It names her way a "special kind of holiness" (« peculiare sanctitatis genus »).
- Source: AAS 13 pp. 449–450; repeated almost word for word in the 1925 *de tuto* decree, AAS 17 pp. 200–201
- Source type: official | Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « de puella enim agitur, quae brevem vitam in claustro absconditam egit, paucis cognita, donec vixit »
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: What set her apart (official statement).
### F-OM-044 — Benedict XV's allocution of 14 Aug 1921: "the secret of sanctity"
- Claim: In the Consistorial Hall, after the decree was read, Benedict XV said all who know "little Thérèse" acclaim her life as "the type of all the virtues of spiritual childhood. There lies the secret of sanctity, not only for the people of France, but for all the Faithful"; he explained spiritual childhood with the image of a small child running to its mother's arms; he said "so rich was she in the science of the saints that she was able to point out to others the true path to salvation"; he said that during the war he himself received many letters from French soldiers and officers who credited her with saving them from death and told of changed lives.
- Source: Taylor 1926/30 PDF 275, 282–295 (English text); Taylor 1924 PDF 449–450
- Source type: official (in English translation) | Rights: public-domain
- Other sources: Laveille PDF 426 calls it the "admirable Discourse" that pronounced her method "the secret of holiness". Taylor calls it Benedict XV's "Nunc dimittis" (he died 22 Jan 1922).
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high for the English text; the Italian original is not in AAS 13 (searched).
### F-OM-047 — Cardinal Vico: "We must hasten … otherwise we shall be forestalled by the voice of the people"
- Claim: Taylor reports that Cardinal Vico, Prefect of Rites, said some years before: "We must hasten to glorify the little Saint, otherwise we shall be forestalled by the voice of the people"; and at the Lisieux Carmel, when in France for the consecration of the Montmartre basilica (1919): "Were we living in the early centuries of the Church, when the Servants of God were beatified by acclamation, Sœur Thérèse would long ago have ranked among the Blessed."

- Source type: popular (reported speech) | Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium
### F-OM-048 — Grave visits before 1923: about 80,000 pilgrims a year
- Claim: Taylor says it was reckoned that on average 80,000 pilgrims prayed at her grave each year; some climbed the steep hill barefoot, rosary in hand; "It is like praying at Lourdes." In 1903, when Taylor first knelt there, he found "the apathy of Lisieux".
- Source: Taylor 1926/30 PDF 274, 420
- Source type: eyewitness (Taylor) | Rights: public-domain

## Part D. Beatification (1922–1923)
### F-OM-049 — Six cures sent to Rome; two chosen; six medical experts
- Claim: Taylor says six cures were submitted and two selected. The 1923 decree says the Congregation learned from "six experts, three for each healing", appointed by the Congregation, that both were organic illnesses with a definite anatomical and pathological lesion, which nature could never have restored in that way.

- Source type: official | Rights: public-domain
